# Pessimistic

Inputs and output for the Pessimistic pass. Sub-sectors and AI Role are from `data/screener/AI Role/`; Current growth and Gross margin vs 5-yr low are from `data/screener/Current Growth/` (the margin gap from the screener, mid-November 2026; for Nubank, from its FMP statements). A dash means not available. RELX, LSEGY and SBGSY report half-yearly: their figures use their last two half-years as the trailing year.

| # | Symbol | Company | Sub-sectors | AI Role | Current growth | Gross margin vs 5-yr low | Pessimistic |

| 1 | AAON | AAON, Inc. | cooling | AAON makes HVAC equipment, and the description lists specialized data center cooling, chillers and air handling units among its products and data centers among its markets, sold through divisions that include BasX. The news ties its AI exposure to BASX, described as its line of data center products: BASX sales rose 218.3% to $397.2M in Q3 2026, more than half of record revenue of $707.4M, with William Blair citing repeat hyperscaler orders and new customer wins, and backlog rose 40.5% to $1.9B. By those figures, data center cooling is now the larger and faster-growing part of the business. | Sales grew 71.5% over the trailing twelve months and 84.1% year over year in the latest quarter, so the most recent quarter is running ahead of the annual pace. Growth is speeding up sharply: the annual growth rate rose 62.7 percentage points from about 9% a year ago, and the quarterly growth rate rose 66.7 percentage points from about 17% four quarters earlier, which together point to a steep step-up over the year from a much lower base. Gross margin over the trailing twelve months is 0.3 percentage points below its five-year low, meaning it is below every yearly level of the last five years, so there is no sign that higher prices are driving the growth; it looks more like volume. | -0.3pp | AAON sits in cooling, where the pessimistic scenario hits both kinds of cooling at once: fewer accelerator orders mean fewer racks and less liquid cooling, and fewer finished data centers mean less air cooling. The AI Role places AAON's data center business, BASX, in specialized cooling, chillers and air handling units, so it is exposed through both channels. Air handling and chillers are tied to finished facilities. Any liquid cooling content is tied to rack counts. The exposure is large: BASX was more than half of Q3 2026 revenue at $397.2M of $707.4M, and it grew 218.3% while the company grew 84.1%, so most of the current growth comes from data centers. The repeat hyperscaler orders show that hyperscalers are among its buyers, so one cutting its spending would be felt directly, though the inputs do not show how concentrated its sales are. The main cushion is backlog of $1.9B, up 40.5%, which is more than two and a half times the latest quarter's revenue and may support near-term revenue; the inputs do not say whether those orders can be cancelled or deferred. The rest of the business is HVAC equipment for other buildings, which the scenario does not cover. It is tied to a construction and industrial cycle with its own risks, so it helps but should not be treated as safe; it was also growing far more slowly, judging by the step-up from about 9% annual growth a year ago. Gross margin is 0.3 percentage points below its five-year low, so there is no sign of shortage pricing that could unwind, and earnings would not be expected to fall further than sales because of pricing. Overall, a large share of AAON's demand and most of its recent growth is at risk, because its data center business is exposed to both the liquid and air sides of the cooling slowdown and appears concentrated in hyperscalers; it is cushioned by a sizable backlog of uncertain firmness, a non-AI HVAC business with its own cycle, and margins that leave little pricing to lose. |
| 2 | AMAT | Applied Materials, Inc. | wafers, memory | Applied Materials makes the equipment used to fabricate semiconductors and services chip plants, through its Semiconductor Systems and Applied Global Services segments (description), which makes it an equipment supplier in both wafers and memory; chipmaking equipment and services are its whole business. The news says AI-driven demand for advanced logic, memory and advanced packaging is driving its growth: FQ4 2026 revenue was a record $10.41B (up 53%), FY2026 revenue was $34.45B, management said semiconductor systems was on track for more than 40% growth in 2026, and it projected advanced-packaging revenue growth above 70% and about $2B of eBeam revenue. The news also reports TSMC, Intel, Samsung, Micron and SK hynix among the partners at its $5B EPIC research center and a stake being raised to about 20% in Besi for hybrid bonding, and headlines call it the equipment maker AI chips depend on. | Sales grew 21.4% over the trailing twelve months, but the latest quarter was up 53.1% year over year, well above the annual pace. Growth is accelerating: the annual growth rate rose 17.0 percentage points from about 4% a year ago, and the quarterly growth rate rose 56.5 percentage points, implying the quarter four quarters earlier was down about 3% year over year. Most of the speed-up is recent and has not yet fully shown up in the trailing figure, and part of the latest quarter's jump may reflect comparison with a weak quarter a year ago. Gross margin is 3.5 percentage points above its five-year low, a modest gap that does not suggest the growth is coming mainly from higher prices. | +3.5pp | Applied Materials sits in wafers and memory as an equipment supplier, and chipmaking equipment and services are its whole business. The pessimistic scenario says equipment and materials suppliers follow the output, so their orders slow as wafer and packaging orders follow accelerator orders down. In memory, less memory is bought, the shortage eases and pricing may soften, which would make memory makers less inclined to add capacity; that is an inference from the scenario rather than something it states for equipment. The parts of the business the AI Role ties most closely to AI, advanced packaging (projected to grow above 70%) and the systems growth of more than 40% in 2026, are the ones that would slow first. Current growth also leans on the recent upturn, with the latest quarter up 53.1% against 21.4% over the trailing year, and part of that may reflect a weak quarter a year ago, so much of the growth at risk is recent. The cushions are partial. Applied Global Services, which services chip plants, likely depends on the installed base more than on new orders and may hold up better; this is an inference, and the inputs give no split. Chipmaking for markets outside AI is not covered by the scenario and has its own cycle. Gross margin is 3.5 percentage points above its five-year low, a modest gap, so pricing is not a strong sign that earnings would fall much further than sales. Overall, a substantial part of Applied Materials' demand is at risk, particularly the recent AI-driven acceleration in advanced packaging and memory equipment, with orders slowing as chipmakers' output does; it is cushioned by its services business, chip demand outside AI, and a modest margin gap that limits how much pricing could fall. |
| 3 | AMZN | Amazon.com, Inc. | runtime, processors, data, tools, trust | Amazon runs a global retail business alongside AWS, which the description says provides compute, storage, database, analytics and machine learning services. The news centers its AI exposure on AWS as a cloud provider: AWS revenue grew 37% to $42.2B in Q2, its fastest in 18 quarters, with a backlog of $496B (later cited at $578B) and capacity constrained into 2027, supported by about $220B of capex this year and plans to add two million Nvidia GPUs in 2027-2028; the news also reports that Anthropic has non-cancelable compute commitments with the hyperscalers including Amazon. In processors, the news reports software released to ease adoption of its Trainium chips. Its retail marketplace and third-party seller platform, which the description says serve consumers, sellers and advertisers, generate proprietary data about users and transactions, which places it among the platforms that hold data; the database and analytics services the description lists for AWS also serve customers working with their own data. The same marketplace is a system that shopping agents have to reach to buy anything, which places it in tools: retail coverage reports Amazon blocking Meta's Muse and Grok shopping agents, framed as a fight over who controls agentic commerce. In trust, AWS sells security services and Bedrock Guardrails, which set controls on what AI applications built on it can do; this comes from background knowledge rather than the description or the news. Kindle+ also added AI-narrated audiobooks. | Sales grew 15.3% over the trailing twelve months and 12.1% year over year in the latest quarter, so the latest quarter is a little below the annual pace. The change is mixed: the annual growth rate rose 3.9 percentage points from about 11% a year ago, while the quarterly growth rate fell 1.3 percentage points from about 13% four quarters earlier, which suggests growth picked up earlier but is now steady to slightly softer. Gross margin is 9.3 percentage points above its five-year low, a meaningful gap that may mean part of the growth comes from higher prices rather than more volume, though the low year may simply have been weak. | +9.3pp | Amazon spans five sub-sectors, but most of its AI exposure is AWS as a cloud provider, in runtime. In the pessimistic scenario less inference has to be run if adoption slows or enterprises cap what agents spend, and a lab that fails to raise capital buys less computing from cloud providers. Both apply directly: AWS grew 37% to $42.2B in Q2, and the AI Role ties part of its backlog to Anthropic's compute commitments. That concentration cuts both ways. The commitments are described as non-cancelable, which supports revenue, but the scenario's case of a lab failing to raise capital would put their value in question. The main cushion is AWS's backlog of $496B, later cited at $578B, and capacity constrained into 2027, which suggests demand may be delayed rather than lost for some time if AI demand weakens. The roughly $220B of capex this year and plans for two million more Nvidia GPUs are the other side: if demand falls, that capacity would carry costs that sales do not cover, so earnings could fall further than sales; this is an inference. In processors, Trainium is, from background knowledge, mainly used inside AWS, so it follows AWS demand. In data and tools, the marketplace holds proprietary data and is a system shopping agents must reach, so it would lose agent volume but not its place. Trust services are a small part. Retail is outside the sub-sectors and depends on consumer spending, which has its own cycle; it is large and supports the company's place, but is not safe on its own. Current growth of 15.3% suggests AI is a part rather than most of Amazon's growth. Gross margin is 9.3 percentage points above its five-year low, a meaningful gap; part of it may come from a shift toward AWS and advertising (an inference from background knowledge) rather than shortage pricing, so it is a possible sign. Overall, AWS's AI demand and the return on Amazon's heavy capex are at risk, especially if a large lab customer falters, while a very large contracted backlog, capacity tightness into 2027 and a big retail business outside AI cushion it, leaving a moderate share of the company's total demand at risk but its earnings more exposed than its sales. |
| 4 | ANET | Arista Networks, Inc. | networking | Arista designs and sells cloud networking products, namely Ethernet switching and routing hardware, its extensible operating system and network applications, to customers that the description says include major internet companies, telecom providers and financial firms. The news describes it as an Ethernet networking supplier for AI data centers, and its own release calls it a leader in AI fabric networking. September coverage said its AI-fabrics customer base grew from a handful in 2024 to more than 100 and that it expected at least $3.6B of AI revenue in 2026, about a quarter of its raised 2026 outlook of $13.3B; its 2027 targets of about $18.5B include $7B from AI fabrics. Q3 2026 revenue was a record $3.66B, up 58.5%.<br><br>The news places Arista in all three levels of AI networking. Its core AI fabrics, Ethernet switching that links racks across AI data centers, are scale out. For scale up, it reported the Etherlink SU-144 rack-scale system, which links up to 144 accelerators in one hop and was built through an Ethernet scale-up initiative with AMD, Arm, Broadcom, d-Matrix, Meta, Microsoft and Qualcomm. For scale across, it reported R4 platforms for distributed inference in November, with an executive saying about 30% of its AI business is already scale-across. Headlines frame it as an AI play beyond chips, often paired with Vertiv. | Sales grew 40.7% over the trailing twelve months and 58.5% year over year in the latest quarter, so the latest quarter is running well ahead of the annual pace. Growth is speeding up: the annual growth rate rose 13.0 percentage points from about 28% a year ago, and the quarterly growth rate rose 31.0 percentage points from about 28% four quarters earlier, pointing to high growth that is still accelerating, with most of the pickup in recent quarters. Gross margin is 1.8 percentage points above its five-year low, a small gap that gives no sign the growth depends on higher prices. | +1.8pp | Arista sits in networking, and its AI business is in the parts the pessimistic scenario says may slow most. The scenario says fewer links are bought as accelerator orders fall and fewer data centers are finished, so clusters stop growing larger, and the links between racks and between data centers grow with cluster size. Arista's core AI fabrics link racks across data centers (scale out), and an executive says about 30% of its AI business is already scale across, for distributed inference between sites; both sit where the scenario expects the sharpest slowdown. Its scale-up product, the Etherlink SU-144 rack system, depends on new racks being built, and the scenario says new rack designs may be adopted more slowly. The exposure is meaningful but not the whole company: at least $3.6B of AI revenue is expected in 2026, about a quarter of a $13.3B outlook, but the 2027 target puts AI at $7B of about $18.5B, so most of the expected growth comes from AI. Current growth of 58.5% in the latest quarter, well above the trailing 40.7%, also suggests the recent acceleration is mostly AI. Customer concentration is a risk: the description names major internet companies among its customers, and from background knowledge a few large cloud companies have long been a large share of its sales, so one hyperscaler cutting its spending would be felt directly. The cushions are its business with telecom providers, financial firms and enterprises, which the scenario does not cover and which depends on ordinary networking spending; the inputs give no backlog or contract figures to support near-term revenue. Gross margin is only 1.8 percentage points above its five-year low, so there is no sign of shortage pricing to unwind, and pricing alone would not make earnings fall much further than sales. Overall, the AI quarter of Arista's sales and most of its expected growth are at risk, sitting in the cluster-scale networking the scenario expects to slow most and likely concentrated in a few large buyers; it is cushioned by a larger non-AI networking business and margins that do not depend on scarcity, but not by any contracted backlog shown in the inputs. |

Its AI business is long-term supply to hyperscalers: Google's 20-year uprate deal for 890 MW and 15-year 2,700 MW supply deal, Amazon's 20-year 690 MW deal at Calvert Cliffs, and the Microsoft-backed Crane restart. These are contracts with hyperscalers rather than labs, and their length gives them more protection than the scenario's power bought before a site is finished, though whether they can be undone is not shown. The exposure is narrower than the headlines suggest. Most of a 32,400 MW portfolio sells power to utilities, governments, businesses and households, outside the sub-sector, and the inputs say the Calpine acquisition helped Q3 revenue growth, so part of the reported growth is acquired rather than AI. That wider business depends on power prices, which carry their own commodity risk. The bigger risk in the scenario is to earnings rather than contracted volume. Gross margin is 22.2 percentage points above its five-year low, a very large gap. If expected data center demand fades, wholesale power prices that may have been lifted by it could soften; this is an inference, since the inputs do not say what drove the gap, and the low year may have been weak. Customers including Coherent and Lumentum are paying upfront to secure supply, which commits them and is a cushion, though how firm that backlog is is not known. The larger risk is to earnings. Non-GAAP gross margin was 56.1%, 31.3 percentage points above its five-year low, a very large gap tied in the inputs to indium phosphide being a bottleneck. If demand eases, the bottleneck would loosen and pricing could fall, so earnings would fall much further than sales, though the low year may have been unusually weak. The up to $545M financing to expand capacity at its Chinese subsidiary would add supply just as demand fell, adding to that pressure; this is an inference. Gallium arsenide and germanium substrates for wireless, LEDs and VCSELs are outside the scenario and follow consumer electronics cycles. Overall, most of AXT's growth and an even larger share of its earnings are at risk, because its margins rest on an indium phosphide shortage the scenario would ease; it is cushioned by upfront payments and a large backlog and by gallium arsenide and germanium businesses with their own cycles. |

Gross margin is 30.4 percentage points above its five-year low, a very large gap that may mean a substantial part of the growth is coming from higher prices rather than more volume, which could prove temporary if prices fall back, though the low year may have been unusually weak, so this is a possible sign rather than proof. | +30.4pp | Western Digital sits in memory as the nearest sub-sector for a storage company, now a hard drive maker after spinning off its flash business, per the news. The scenario says less memory and storage are bought as fewer servers are ordered, and the shortage eases and pricing may soften. Its exposure is large: most revenue comes from cloud providers, and revenue grew 55% on hard drive demand, rising prices and higher-capacity drives. Hard drives store the data that training and inference draw on, which keeps accumulating whether or not new accelerators are ordered, so demand may hold better than demand for faster memory; this is an inference, and the scenario does not say it. Concentration in cloud providers means a hyperscaler cutting spending would be felt directly, and neoclouds, cited as broadening demand, are among the buyers likely to pause first. The inputs give no backlog or supply agreements, so the cushion cannot be sized. The larger risk is to earnings. Gross margin is 30.4 percentage points above its five-year low, a very large gap, and the inputs cite rising prices and tight supply adding pricing leverage. If demand eases, the shortage would loosen and pricing could fall, so earnings would fall much further than sales, though the low year may have been unusually weak. Sovereign customers and physical AI are newer sources of demand, also tied to AI. The inputs describe no business outside data center storage after the flash spin-off, so little cushions it outside the scenario. Overall, a meaningful share of Western Digital's demand and a larger share of its earnings are at risk, because its sales are concentrated in cloud providers and its margins rest on a drive shortage the scenario would ease; it is cushioned mainly by stored data that keeps growing, but the inputs show no backlog or long-term agreements. |

The news frames it as a gas and produced water midstream partnership in the Delaware and DJ Basins and contains no AI stories or AI framing.<br><br>Reported items are record gas and produced water throughput in Q3 2026, full-year EBITDA expected above the midpoint of raised guidance (MarketBeat), the Brazos Delaware acquisition, and the Pathfinder Pipeline and North Loving II projects due in 2027, with a Seeking Alpha author estimating 2027 EBITDA of $3.2B. Growth is speeding up: the annual growth rate rose 16.5 percentage points from about 6% a year ago, and the quarterly growth rate rose 17.6 percentage points from about 8% four quarters earlier, which suggests a consistent step-up from a low base over the year. Gross margin over the trailing twelve months is 3.5 percentage points below its five-year low, meaning it is below every yearly level of the last five years, so there is no sign that higher prices are driving the growth, which is more consistent with growth coming from volume. | -3.5pp | Western Midstream sits in power only indirectly, as a gas gathering, processing and transport partnership, the supply chain behind gas-fired data center power. The scenario says less power is needed if fewer data centers are built, which would reduce the expected growth in gas demand from data centers. The inputs contain no AI stories or framing for Western Midstream and no data center customers, so none of its current business is shown to depend on the buildout, and what the scenario would take away is only a share of future gas demand growth that the inputs do not attribute to it. The whole business is outside the scenario. Gathering, compression, processing and transport of gas, NGLs and crude, and produced water gathering and disposal in the Delaware and DJ Basins and elsewhere, depend on producers' drilling and production volumes and on commodity markets, which carry their own risks. Record gas and produced water throughput, the Brazos Delaware acquisition and the Pathfinder and North Loving II projects due in 2027 reflect production growth in its basins rather than AI. Growth stepped up from about 8% in the quarter a year earlier to 25.4%, consistent with volume growth and acquisitions. Gross margin is 3.5 percentage points below its five-year low, so there is no sign of pricing that could unwind, and earnings would not be expected to fall further than sales because of pricing in this scenario. Overall, essentially none of Western Midstream's demand or earnings is at risk in the pessimistic scenario, because the inputs show no link to AI beyond its place in the gas supply chain; its results depend on producers' activity and commodity markets outside the scenario. |
